The applicant estimated the effect of the overall phenotype on PK using the wild-type
phenotype as reference. The applicant’s analysis indicated a statistically-significant decrease in
AUC and Cmax (19.5% and 23.4%, respectively) in the elevated phenotype, a statisticallysignificant increase in AUC (25.6%) with no increase in Cmax in the reduced phenotype. No
statistically-significant differences were observed in the mixed phenotype. In addition, the
analysis showed the 90% confidence interval for the active moiety AUC ratios across
phenotypes contained 0 signifying no evidence of phenotype-mediated differences in AUC. The
Applicant concluded that abrocitinib parent exposures were comparable across overall
phenotypes.

(b) (4)

(b) (4)

Are the CYP2C19 and CYP2C9 phenotypes inferred from genotypes appropriate?
The Applicant has evaluated the common alleles in CYP2C19 and CYP2C9 that have established
functional impact on each enzyme. The CYP2C19 and CYP2C9 genotype-inferred assignments of
290
Reference ID: 4920256

phenotype is consistent with standard assignments of ultrarapid, rapid, extensive (now normal
[PMID: 27441996]), intermediate and poor metabolizer phenotypes based on the functional
status of CYP2C19 *1, *2, *3, *4, and *17 and CYP2C9 *1, *2, and *3 alleles. However, in the
combined classification of CYP2C19/CYP2C9 phenotypes, the Applicant deviates from the
genotype-inferred phenotype assignment of a CYP2C9 *2/*2 as an intermediate metabolizer
and assigns CYP2C9*2/*2 as a poor metabolizer when combined with CYP2C19 phenotypes in
the overall phenotype categories. This inconsistency impacted (n=1) subject (Table 116).
Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations based
on intrinsic patient factors?

Regression analysis from NCA data (ncamf.xpt) of 648 records. 331 records were excluded for PK of other moieties,
absence of metabolizer phenotype or AUC. Additional 15 records were excluded due to an effect in exposure
identified in Phase 2 tablet. From the remaining 316 records, doses higher than 200mg or given BID were excluded
due to non-linear dose proportionality. Ultimately, 250 records (single dose) in healthy subjects treated with
abrocitinib alone were included and used in summary statistics.* Subject misclassified as EM/PM by Applicant was
recategorized and included as EM/IM.

The evaluation of CYP2C19 phenotypes independently in single dose studies indicated that
CYP2C19 IM and PM phenotypes had a 31% and 126% increase in AUC respectively while
CYP2C19 RM and UM phenotypes showed a 14% and 30% reduction in exposure respectively
when compared to the EM phenotype. CYP2C9 IM and PM phenotypes showed a 23% and 68%
increase in exposure respectively when compared to the CYP2C9 EM phenotype.
Of the CYP2C19/2C9 combined the phenotypes, UM/EM and RM/EM phenotypes were
associated with a 24% and 15% decrease in AUC compared to EM/EM phenotype. The PM/EM
combination (n=6) showed a 144% increase in exposure when compared to the EM/EM
combination. In comparison, the EM/PM combination (with a similarly small sample size n=4)
increased by 77%, reflecting the predominance of the contribution of CYP2C19 to the
metabolism of abrocitinib. Similarly, abrocitinib AUC increased by 48% in IM/EM compared to
EM/EM however, the increase in AUC was 23% in EM/IM phenotypes suggesting the decreased
CYP2C19 enzyme activity in the IM/EM combination made more impact on the exposure than
the decreased CYP2C9 activity in the EM/IM phenotype combination.
In the overall phenotype categories as proposed by the applicant, the mixed and reduced
overall phenotypes showed a 48% and 41% increase in exposure respectively while elevated
overall phenotype showed a 18% reduction in exposure when compared to wild type. The
variability observed between the combined phenotypes that constitute the reduced overall
phenotype (Figure 33) indicates that the overall categorization diminishes the impact of the
CYP2C19 PM phenotype when combined with other phenotypes.
Figure 33. Dose-Normalized AUC in Single Dose Studies by Combined CYP2C19 and CYP2C9
and Overall Phenotype Category

Reference ID: 4920256

* indicates statistical significance; sex, fed, age, weight, race had significant impact on AUC which might not be balanced among
different metabolizer phenotypes. Increased AUC was associated with female sex, increased age, fed state and race
Asian/Other > White/Black > unknown. Increased BW was associated with decreased AUC

In multiple dose studies, no subjects with CYP2C19 PM phenotype were in the analysis and the
impact either independently or in combination with CYP2C9 phenotypes could not be evaluated
(Table 117).The EM/IM phenotype in multiple dose studies demonstrated a 66% decrease in
AUC when compared to the EM/EM phenotype. The sample size (n=1) limits the interpretation
of this finding, which is in contrast to the EM/IM phenotype in single dose studies that showed
a 23% increase in AUC.

Regression analysis from NCA data (ncamf.xpt) of 648 records. 331 records were excluded for PK of other moieties, absence of
metabolizer phenotype or AUC. Additional 15 records were excluded due to an effect in exposure identified in Phase 2 tablet.
From the remaining 316 records, doses higher than 200mg or given BID were excluded due to non-linear dose proportionality.
Ultimately 28 records (multiple doses) in healthy subjects treated with abrocitinib alone were included and used in summary
statistics.

Other possible combinations not reflected in the pool of analyzed subjects include the scenarios
of PM/IM and PM/PM combination. The mean increase observed in the EM/PM phenotypes,
though 77% higher than the reference phenotype, is characterized by high variability in a small
sample size therefore undermining a compelling rationale for CYP2C9 PM phenotype-directed
293
Reference ID: 4920256

dosing recommendation. It is expected that the dose recommendation for CYP2C19 PM
phenotypes mitigates exposure differences in PM/PM phenotypes. In a September 17, 2021
response to an information request to detail the adverse event profile of CYP2C19 poor
metabolizers in the abrocitinib development program, the applicant noted CYP2C19 genotyping
information was not available from Phase 2 and Phase 3 studies. A large variability in exposure
was observed in AD patients (1840 to 39500 ng*h/mL for 200 mg QD) and based on the PK/PD
analysis, significant thrombocytopenia with serious clinical concerns is not expected for
patients with baseline platelet counts >=210·000/µL at the dose of 200 mg. (See Section 16.5.2
for details). In the context of increasing the recommended abrocitinib dose in non-responders,
dose increase to 100 mg may be permitted in patients known to be CYP2C19 poor metabolizers
who do not respond to the initial recommended 50 mg dose.

1. Summary
In this submission the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in
Crl:WI(Han) rats and one in rasH2 (tg/wt) mice. These studies were intended to assess the
carcinogenic potential of Abrocitinib, when administered orally by gavage at appropriate drug
levels for 104 weeks in rats and 26 weeks in mice.
Rat Study: For male rats, the survival analyses showed a statistically significant dose
response relationship in mortality across the vehicle control group and treated groups in male
rats (p-value=0.0015 for likelihood ratio test, p-value=0.0115 for log-rank test). The pairwise
comparisons showed a statistically significant difference in mortality between the vehicle
control group and the 30 mg/kg/day group in male rats (p-value=0.0017 for both likelihood
ratio test and log-rank test).
For female rats, the pairwise comparisons showed a statistically significant difference in
mortality between the vehicle control group and the 10 mg/kg/day group in female rats (pvalue=0.0026 for likelihood ratio test, p-value=0.0023 for log-rank test).
Tumor analysis:
1. For female rats, the pairwise comparison of the B-thymoma in thymus(pvalue=<0.01) between the vehicle control group and the 10 mg/kg/day group is
significant at 0.01 level of significance.
2. For female rats, the pairwise comparison of the B-thymoma in thymus(pvalue=<0.01) between the vehicle control group and the 30 mg/kg/day group is
significant at 0.01 level of significance.

2. Background
In this submission the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in
Crl:WI(Han) rats and one in rasH2 (tg/wt) mice. These studies were intended to assess the
carcinogenic potential of Abrocitinib, when administered orally by gavage at appropriate drug
levels for 104 weeks in rats and 26 weeks in mice. Results of this review have been discussed
with the reviewing pharmacologist Dr. Jiaqin Yao. This review analyzed the SAS data sets of
these studies received from the sponsor on June 29, 2020 via NDA213871/0001.
In this review the phrase "dose response relationship" refers to the linear component of the
effect of treatment, and not necessarily to a strictly increasing or decreasing mortality or tumor
incidence rate as the dose increases.

3. Rat Study
Two separate experiments were conducted, one in males and one in females. In each of
these two experiments there were three treated groups and one vehicle control group. Two
hundred and forty Crl:WI(Han) rats of each sex were randomly assigned to the four groups in
equal size of 60 rats per group. The dose levels for treated groups were 3, 10 and 30
mg/kg/day. The rats in the vehicle control group received the vehicle(0.5% (w/v)
methylcellulose, 4000 cPs/0.1% (v/v) polysorbate 80 (Tween 80) in reverse osmosis water).
The study for the rats was designed to continue for up to 104 weeks. In accordance with
study termination criteria, all surviving male rats were sacrificed during Week 105.

Sponsor's analyses

3.1.1. Survival analysis

Reference ID: 4717348

NDA

(b) (4)

Page 9 of 40

Tests to compare survival were conducted, with a 2-sided risk for increasing and decreasing
mortality with dose. Tests were conducted for dose response along with pairwise
comparisons for each dosed group against the control group using Kaplan-Meier productlimit estimates (Kaplan and Meier, 1958), along with log-rank and Wilcoxon tests (Peto et
al., 1980). These were conducted using the LIFETEST procedure in SAS (SAS, 2002-2008).
The dosed groups were included as the stratum. Animals with a death or euthanasia status
recorded as a scheduled euthanasia or accidental death were censored in the analysis. The
time to death or euthanasia (in weeks, calculation detailed as follows) was the dependent
variable.
Sponsor’s findings: Sponsor’s analysis showed the numbers (percents) of survival were 45
(75%), 39 (65%), 37 (62%), and 28 (47%) in vehicle control, 3 mg/kg/day, 10 mg/kg/day and
30 mg/kg/day dose groups, respectively in males and 25 (42%), 32 (53%), 41 (68%), and 31
(52%) in vehicle controlontrol, 3 mg/kg/day, 10 mg/kg/day and 30 mg/kg/day dose groups,
respectively in females.
The sponsor concluded that, For males, the test for an increasing trend with dose was
significant; P = 0.0012 and P = 0.0013 for the Log-Rank and Wilcoxon tests, respectively.
The high-dose group (30 mg/kg/day) had higher mortality than control (32 of 60 versus 15
of 60 in control), with P = 0.0017 and P = 0.0022 for the Log-Rank and Wilcoxon tests,
respectively.
For females, the trend test was not significant. Pairwise tests detected that the control
group had higher mortality (35 out of 60 animals died) than the intermediate-dose group (10
mg/kg/day) (19 of 60 animals died), with P = 0.0033 and P = 0.0042 for the Log-Rank and
Wilcoxon tests, respectively.

3.1.2. Tumor data analysis
Tests to compare tumor incidence were conducted with a 1-sided risk for increasing
incidence with dose. Tests were conducted for a dose response along with pairwise
comparisons for each dosed group against the control group.

Reference ID: 4717348

NDA

(b) (4)

Page 10 of 40

Unadjusted P-values were reported for tumors. An indication of a possible dose effect was
assessed on the basis of whether the tumor is a rare or common type, in line with the
current FDA guidelines (Food and Drug Administration, 2001) and Expanded Statistical
Decision Rules for Interpretations of Results of Rodent Carcinogenicity Studies of
Pharmaceuticals (Lin and Rahman, 2019). The Study Pathologist determined whether a
tumor type was rare or common, based on the incidence rate in the site-specific
background historical data. If the concurrent control rate in the study is >1%, the tumor was
considered common, regardless of background historical rates.
The Study Pathologist determined which tumor combinations are analyzed, using the
criteria for combination in Guidelines for Combining Neoplasms for Evaluation of Rodent
Carcinogenicity Studies (McConnell et al., 986) as a guide. Incidences of multiple-organ and
combined neoplastic findings, such as hemangioma, fibrosarcoma, and endometrial stromal
polyp, were counted by animal, not tissue type.
Sponsor’s findings: The sponsor concluded that for females, the B-Fibroma, Skin/Subcutis
was statistically significant when comparing the 10 mg/kg dose group with the vehicle
control.

3.2.

Reviewer's analyses

To verify sponsor’s analyses and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing
pharmacologist, this reviewer independently performed survival and tumor data analyses. Data
used in this reviewer's analyses were provided by the sponsor electronically on June 29, 2020
via NDA213871/0001.

3.2.1. Survival analysis
The survival distributions of animals in four groups were estimated by the Kaplan-Meier
product limit method. The dose response relationship and homogeneity of survival
distributions were tested for the vehicle controls, low, medium and high dose groups using the
Likelihood Ratio test and the Log-Rank test. The intercurrent mortality data are given in
Tables 7 and 8 in the appendix for males and females, respectively. The Kaplan-Meier curves
for survival rate are given in Figures 1 and 2 in the appendix for males and females,

Reference ID: 4717348

NDA

(b) (4)

Page 11 of 40

respectively. Results of the tests for dose response relationship and homogeneity of survivals,
are given in Tables 9 and 10 in the appendix for males and females, respectively.
Reviewer’s findings: This reviewer’s analysis showed the numbers (percents) of survival
were 45 (75%), 39 (65%), 37 (62%), and 28 (47%) in vehicle control, 3 mg/kg/day, 10
mg/kg/day and 30 mg/kg/day dose groups, respectively in males and 25 (42%), 32 (53%),
41 (68%), and 31 (52%) in vehicle controlontrol, 3 mg/kg/day, 10 mg/kg/day and 30
mg/kg/day dose groups, respectively in females.
For male rats, the survival analyses showed a statistically significant dose response
relationship in mortality across the vehicle control group and treated groups in male rats (pvalue=0.0015 for likelihood ratio test, p-value=0.0115 for log-rank test). The pairwise
comparisons showed a statistically significant difference in mortality between the vehicle
control group and the 30 mg/kg/day group in male rats (p-value=0.0017 for both likelihood
ratio test and log-rank test).
For female rats, the survival analyses didn’t show any statistically significant dose response
relationship in mortality across the vehicle control group and treated groups. The pairwise
comparisons showed a statistically significant difference in mortality between the vehicle
control group and the 10 mg/kg/day group in female rats (p-value=0.0026 for likelihood ratio
test, p-value=0.0023 for log-rank test).

3.2.2. Tumor data analysis
The tumor data were analyzed for the positive dose response relationships and the positive
pairwise comparison increases between each of the treated groups with control group. Both
the dose response relationship tests and pairwise comparisons were performed using the
Poly-K method described in the paper of Bailer and Portier (1988) and Bieler and Williams
(1993). In this method an animal that lives the full study period ( wmax ) or dies before the
terminal sacrifice but develops the tumor type being tested gets a score of sh =1. An animal

 w 
that dies at week wh without a tumor before the end of the study gets a score of sh =  h 
 wmax 
< 1. The adjusted group size is defined as Σ sh . As an interpretation, an animal with score sh

k

=1 can be considered as a whole animal while an animal with score sh < 1 can be considered

Reference ID: 4717348

NDA

(b) (4)

Page 12 of 40

as a partial animal. The adjusted group size Σ sh is equal to N (the original group size) if all
animals live up to the end of the study or if each animal that dies before the terminal sacrifice
develops at least one tumor, otherwise the adjusted group size is less than N. These adjusted
group sizes were then used for the dose response relationship (or the pairwise) tests using the
Cochran-Armitage test. One critical point for Poly-k test is the choice of the appropriate value
of k, which depends on the tumor incidence pattern with the increased dose. For long term 104
week standard rat and mouse studies, a value of k=3 is suggested in the literature. Hence, this
reviewer used k=3 for the analysis of this data. For the calculation of p-values the exact
permutation method was used. The tumor rates and the p-values for the positive dose
response relationship tests and pairwise comparisons are listed in Tables 11 and 12 in the
appendix for male and female rats, respectively.
Adjustment for multiple testing: For the chronic study in rats, the adjustment of multiple
testing of the dose response relationship for a submission with one chronic rat study and
one transgenic mouse study, the more recently revised draft (January, 2013) FDA guidance
for the carcinogenicity studies suggests the use of test levels α =0.005 for common tumors
and α=0.025 for rare tumors for the chronic rat study. For pairwise comparisonsfor the
chronic rat study in the above type of submission with one chronic rat study and one
transgenic mouse study, the same guidance document suggests the use of test levels α
=0.01 for common tumors and α =0.05 for rare tumors for the chronic rat study.
It should be noted that the FDA guidance for multiple testing for dose response relationship
is based on a publication by Lin and Rahman (1998). In this work the authors investigated
the use of this rule for Peto analysis. However, in a later work Rahman and Lin (2008)
showed that this rule for multiple testing for dose response relationship is also suitable for
Poly-K tests.
Reviewer’s findings: The tumor types in Tables 2 below showed p-values less than or equal
to 0.05 in the trend tests and the tests for pairwise comparisons between the vehicle and
the treated groups for female rats. Based on the above criterion for multiple testing
adjustment, we make the folloing statistical conclusions:
3. For female rats, the pairwise comparison of the B-thymoma in thymus(pvalue=<0.01) between the vehicle control group and the 10 mg/kg/day group is
significant at 0.01 level of significance.

Reference ID: 4717348

(b) (4)

NDA

Page 13 of 40

4. For female rats, the pairwise comparison of the B-thymoma in thymus(pvalue=<0.01) between the vehicle control group and the 30 mg/kg/day group is
significant at 0.01 level of significance.

4. Mouse Study
Two separate experiments were conducted, one in males and one in females. In each of
these two experiments there were three treated groups, one vehicle control group and one
positive control group. One hundred Transgenic mice of each sex were randomly assigned
to the treated and vehicle control group in equal size of 25 mice per group. The dose levels
for treated groups were 10, 20, and 60 mg/kg/day for males and females. The mice in the
vehicle control group received the vehicle (0.5% (w/v) methylcellulose,
4000 cPs/0.1% (v/v) polysorbate 80 (Tween® 80) in reverse osmosis water). The mice in the
positive control group received 75 mg/kg/day N-methyl-N-nitrosourea (MNU) via
intraperitoneal injection once on Day 1 of the dosing phase at a dose volume of 10 mL/kg.

Sponsor's analyses

4.1.1. Survival analysis
The sponsor used the same survival analysis methods used for the rats study in this mouse
study.
Sponsor’s findings: The sponsor’s analysis showed that the numbers (percents) of survival
were 25 (100%), 23 (92%), 24 (96%), and 22 (66%) in male mice, and 24 (96%), 25 (100%), 24
(96%), and 17 (68%) in female mice in vehicol control, low, medium, and high dose groups,
respectively.
The sponsor concluded that, for males, there were no statistically significant differences in
the incidence of mortality amongst the groups. For females, the trend test was statistically
significant, with P = <.0001 and P = <.0001 for the Log-Rank and Wilcoxon tests,

Reference ID: 4717348

NDA

(b) (4)

Page 15 of 40

respectively. The high-dose group (75 mg/kg/day) had a statistically significant higher
incidence of mortality than that of the control group (8 of 25 in the high-dose group versus 1
of 25 in the control group), with P = 0.0207 and P = 0.0198 for the Log-Rank and Wilcoxon
tests, respectively.

4.1.2. Tumor data analysis
The sponsor used the same tumor data analysis methods used for the rat study in this
mouse study
Sponsor’s findings: The sponsor concluded that For both males and females, there were no
statistically significant differences in tumor incidence amongst the groups.

4.2.

Reviewer's analyses

To verify sponsor’s analyses and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing
pharmacologist, this reviewer independently performed survival and tumor data analyses. Data
used in this reviewer's analyses were provided by the sponsor electronically on June 29, 2020
via NDA213871/0001.

4.2.1. Survival analysis
The survival distributions of three treated groups, one vehical control group, and one positive
control group were estimated using the Kaplan-Meier product limit method. The dose
response relationship in survival was tested using the likelihood ratio test and the homogeneity
of survival distributions was tested using the log-rank test. The Kaplan-Meier curves for
survival rates are given in Figures 3 and 4 in the appendix for male and female mice,
respectively. The intercurrent mortality data are given in Tables 13 and 14 in the appendix for
male and female mice, respectively. Results of the tests for dose response relationship and
homogeneity of survivals among the vehicle control and three treated groups are given in
Tables 15 and 16 in the appendix for male and female mice, respectively.
Reviewer’s findings: This reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers (percents) of survival

Reference ID: 4717348

NDA

(b) (4)

Page 16 of 40

were 25 (100%), 23 (92%), 24 (96%), 22 (66%) and 0 (0%) in male mice, and 24 (96%), 25
(100%), 24 (96%), 17 (68%) and 2 (8%) in female mice in vehicol control, low, medium, high
dose groups and positive control group, respectively.
For male mice, the survival analyses didn’t show any statistically significant dose response
relationship in mortality across the vehicle control group and treated groups. The pairwise
comparisons did not show any statistically positive significant differences in mortality between
the vehicle control group and each of the treated groups.
For female mice, the survival analyses showed a statistically significant dose response
relationship in mortality across the vehicle control group and treated groups in male rats (pvalue=0.0004 for likelihood ratio test, p-value=0.0002 for log-rank test). The pairwise
comparisons showed a statistically significant difference in mortality between the vehicle
control group and the 60 mg/kg/day group in male rats (p-value=0.007 for both likelihood ratio
test, p-value=0.0103 forlog-rank test).
The pairwise comparisons showed a statistically significant difference in mortality between the
vehicle control group and the positive control group in both male and female rats (pvalue<0.0001 for both likelihood ratio test, p-value<0.0001 forlog-rank test).

4.2.2. Tumor data analysis
The reviewer used the same tumor data analysis methods for the rat study in this mouse
study.
The tumor rates and the p-values for the positive dose response relationship tests and
pairwise comparisons between vehicle control and three treated groups, and between vehicle
control and positive control are listed in Tables 17, 18, 19, and 20 in the appendix for male and
female mice, respectively.
Adjustment for multiple testing: For the chronic study in rats, the adjustment of multiple
testing of the dose response relationship for a submission with one chronic rat study and
one transgenic mouse study, the more recently revised draft (January, 2013) FDA guidance

Reference ID: 4717348

NDA

(b) (4)

Page 17 of 40

for the carcinogenicity studies suggests the use of test levels α =0.05 for both common
tumors and rare tumors for the mouse study. For pairwise, the same guidance document
suggests the use of test levels α =0.05 for both common tumors and rare tumors for the
mouse study.
It should be noted that the FDA guidance for multiple testing for dose response relationship
is based on a publication by Lin and Rahman (1998). In this work the authors investigated
the use of this rule for Peto analysis. However, in a later work Rahman and Lin (2008)
showed that this rule for multiple testing for dose response relationship is also suitable for
Poly-K tests.
Reviewer’s findings: The tumor types in Tables 5 and 6 below showed p-values less than or
equal to 0.05 in the tests for pairwise comparisons between vehicle and positive control
groups for male mice and female mice, respectively.

Reviewer’s findings: Based on the criteria of adjustment for multiple testing discussed in
the mouse data analysis section, we make the following statistical conclusions:
3. For male mice, the pairwise comparisons between the vehicle control and the
positive control group showed statistically significant increase in incidence of Mmalignant lymphoma, hemolympho-reticu (p-value<0.001), B-papilloma, squamous
cell, skin/subcutis (p-value<0.001), B-papilloma, squamous cell, stomach nonglandu
(p-value<0.001) and M-carcinoma, squamous cell, stomach nonglandu (pvalue=0.004)
4. For female mice, the pairwise comparisons between the vehicle control and the
positive control group showed statistically significant increase in incidence of Mmalignant lymphoma, hemolympho-reticu (p-value<0.001), B-papilloma, squamous
cell, skin/subcutis (p-value=0.0452), B-papilloma, squamous cell, stomach
nonglandu (p-value<0.001) and M-carcinoma, squamous cell, stomach nonglandu
(p-value=0.0452)

3 RECOMMENDATIONS FOR FUTURE STUDIES
For future clinical trials in this indication, we recommend sponsors consider the following when
selecting appropriate anchors scales for use in anchor-based methods:
• Selected anchor scales should be associated with the target COA endpoint in a way that
addresses the question of clinical meaningfulness of the target COA endpoint.
•

The anchor scale should be easier to interpret than the COA endpoint itself and
meaningful to patients. The anchor scale’s response categories should be distinct and
non-overlapping and should represent meaningful differences among adjacent response
categories.

•

The anchor scale’s recall period should be consistent with the assessment time period of
the prespecified endpoint to the extent possible. Additionally, the selected anchors should
be assessed at comparable time points as the target COA endpoint but completed by the
respondent after the target COA in the order of assessments.

•

The anchor scale should be plainly understood by respondents in the context of use; you
should consider testing the draft anchor item(s) including their response categories in
cognitive interviews.

Early engagement with FDA during drug development on COA is highly encouraged.
Qualitative research can also provide useful data in interpreting clinically meaningful withinpatient change and can supplement quantitative methods, such as anchor-based analyses. We
recommend that sponsors review the Guidance for Industry Patient-Reported Outcome
Measures: Use in Medical Product Development to Support Labeling Claims 2 and FDA
Discussion Document: Incorporating Clinical Outcome Assessments into Endpoints for
Regulatory Decision-Making 3.

5.2 Clinical Trial Design
The applicant conducted three pivotal phase 3 trials (Studies B7451012, B7451013 and
B7451029). The clinical trial design of these studies is described as follows:
• Studies B7451012 and B7451013 are identical randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled, parallel group, multicenter phase 3 studies to evaluate the efficacy and safety
of abrocitinib monotherapy in subjects aged 12 years and older, with moderate-to-severe
AD. Both studies were monotherapy trials.
•

Study B7451029 is a randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, placebo-controlled,
parallel group, multicenter phase 3 study investigating the efficacy and safety of
abrocitinib and dupilumab in comparison with placebo in adult subjects on background
topical therapy, with moderate-to-severe AD. This study was a trial with background
topical therapy.

In Study B7451012, a total of 387 subjects were included, while in Study B7451013, a total of
391 subjects were included. Subjects were randomized 2:2:1 to receive abrocitinib 200 mg QD,
abrocitinib 100 mg QD, or placebo. The trials consisted of a screening period (28 days), a 12week placebo-controlled, double-blind, treatment period and a 4-week follow-up period.
In Study B7451029, a total of 838 subjects were included. Subjects were randomized 4:4:4:1 to
receive abrocitinib 200 mg QD, abrocitinib 100 mg QD, dupilumab 300mg Q2W, or placebo.
The trial consisted of a screening period (28 days), a 20-week treatment period and a 4-week
follow-up period. The first part of this treatment period consisted of a 16-week randomized,
double-blind, placebo-controlled, double-dummy treatment period with subjects receiving both
injectable and oral investigational products. Following Week 16, subjects previously receiving
only placebo would receive abrocitinib 100 mg or 200 mg QD as per their randomized
allocation. Subjects previously receiving abrocitinib 100 mg or 200 mg QD continued on their
respective dose. Subjects previously receiving dupilumab would take oral placebo.
Refer to the Clinical review and final study reports for more details on the clinical trial design.

5.5 Clinical Outcome Assessment(s)
5.5.1 Clinical Outcome Assessment Description(s)
Pruritus and Symptoms Assessment for Atopic Dermatitis (PSAAD)
The PSAAD is an 11-item PRO instrument designed to assess the severity of AD signs and
symptoms. Each item is rated on an 11-point NRS, ranging from 0 (“Not/No [symptom or
sign]”) to 10 (“Extremely [sign or symptom]”). The recall period is the previous 24 hours. See
Appendix B for a copy of the instrument (paper version).
Reviewer’s comment(s):
While not the subject of this review, the applicant is also proposing labeling claims from the
following instrument(s):
Peak Pruritis Numerical Rating Scale (PP-NRS)
The PP-NRS is a single-item PRO instrument designed to assess itch intensity at its worst. Itch
intensity is rated on an 11-point NRS, ranging from 0 (“No itch”) to 10 (“Worst itch
imaginable”). The recall period is the previous 24 hours. See Appendix A for a copy of the
instrument (paper version).
Night Time Itch Scale Severity Item (NTIS Severity)
The NTIS is a single-item PRO instrument designed to assess nighttime itch intensity at its worst.
Itch intensity is rated on an 11-point NRS, ranging from 0 (“No itch”) to 10 (“Worst itch
imaginable”). The recall period is “during the most recent night’s sleep”. See Appendix C for a
copy of the instrument (paper version).

The conceptual frameworks for PSAAD is shown in Figure 1.
Figure 1. Conceptual Framework for PSAAD

5.5.3 Scoring Algorithm
PSAAD
The PSAAD generates a total score, which ranges from 0 to 10, with higher scores indicating
greater levels of AD symptom severity.
The total score is calculated by summing the item scores and dividing by the number of items
with non-missing data (minimum = 6 items). The total score, therefore, is equivalent to the mean
of the item scores. A minimum of 6 completed items is required to calculate a total score; if
there are less than 6 completed items the score is set to missing.
Reviewer’s comment(s): The applicant applied and pre-specified the "half-rule” method which
requires at least 50% of the items on PSAAD to be completed. If a patient completed at least half
of the PSAAD items, the half-rule procedure of imputation takes the mean of a patient’s observed
responses on the scale. The half-rule method imputes a person-specific estimate for each missing
item based on the average score across completed items on the same scale from the same
person; therefore, this imputation method does not depend on data collected from other subjects,
other trials, or even other scales from the same subject.
The applicant did not provide a rationale for not weighting any of the items. However, it is noted
that the approach to this condition is similar to that of psoriasis in that when a total score is
used, items were given equal weight (see past drug labels for ixekizumab, brodalumab,
risankizumab-rzaa, and guselkumab; note that either a disease-specific instrument or an itch
NRS was used in the labels).
Because even a single daily measurement on the PSAAD total score has demonstrated
acceptable test–retest reliability, the applicant decided that weekly average PSAAD scores were
based on all available data for the 7-day week regardless of how many days the PSAAD score
was completed.
5.5.4 Content Validity
PSAAD
The applicant completed the following instrument development activities to evaluate the content
validity of the PSAAD:
• Literature review,
• Expert input (consensus meeting), and
• Patient input (concept elicitation and cognitive interviews).
A summary of the findings from the qualitative research is shown below. For more details on the
methodology and results of these activities, see the PRO evidence Dossier.
Literature Review:
• A total of 17 articles were reviewed, which included 17 instruments.
• Out of the 17 instruments, there were no instruments identified that comprehensively
assessed all relevant AD symptoms or impacts. Furthermore, no identified instruments
provided sufficient face and content validity.

Expert Input:
• Ten core signs/symptoms were identified by the experts after reviewing findings from the
literature review:
o Pruritus (itching)
o Pain
o Dryness of skin (xerosis)
o Flaking of skin
o Cracking of skin
o Redness of skin
o Bleeding of skin
o Weeping/oozing of skin
o Tightness of skin
o Swelling/inflammation of skin (edema)
These signs/symptoms were included in a draft instrument.
Reviewer’s comment(s): Thickened skin and discoloration were not included in the first draft of
the instrument, while two additional concepts were added to the instrument: sleep disturbance
and activities of daily living.
Patient input:
• The total sample for the concept elicitation interviews consisted of 30 participants with
AD (15 adult participants ≥18 years and 15 adolescent participants aged 12 to 17 years).
o A total of 13 distinct symptoms were reported by participants in the 30 interviews.
Of these symptoms, itch was the primary symptom of AD that was reported
spontaneously by all 30 participants, while skin thickening was the least
frequently reported symptom by participants (n=11/15).
o Itch was reported most commonly by participants as the most frequently
experienced, most severe and most bothersome symptom of AD.
o After itch, dryness (n=6/28, 21%) and redness (n=5/28, 18%) were the next most
frequently reported symptoms.
o After itch, dryness (8/28) and cracking (5/28) were the next most bothersome
symptoms.
o Itching and dry skin were reported to be relevant by all participants (100%).
o Symptoms of redness (87%), bumpy skin (81%), pain (73%), flaky skin (73%),
bleeding (67%), discoloration (63%) and swelling (60%) were more relevant to
the sample compared to tight skin (43%), cracked skin (40%) and fluid (33%)
which was only relevant to a small proportion of participants.
Reviewer’s comment(s): Most AD symptoms were reported by a similar number of adult and
combined adolescent participants; however, cracking, fluid, tightness and thickening were all
reported by slightly more adult participants than adolescents.
•

The total sample for the cognitive interviews consisted of 30 participants with AD (15
adult participants ≥18 years and 15 adolescent participants aged 12 to 17 years).

o Items and instructions of the AD symptom diary were generally well understood
(over 93% for each item) and consistently interpreted across all age groups. Of the
few participants (less than 5% for each item) who did not appear to understand an
item, this was largely due to them never having experienced the symptom

scores across each of the 12 symptom severity items were 3.45 or less and
participants utilized between 70-100% of the response continuum across the 12
severity items. Zero was the most frequently selected response option across all
severity items, while response option 10 was only selected for three of the 12
items: dry skin, bumpy skin and tight skin. The mean scores across each of the 12
symptom bother items were 3.32 or less. Participants utilized between 80% and
100% of the response continuum across the 12 bother items. Zero was the most
frequently selected response option across all bother items, while response option
10 was selected for six of the 12 items: itch, dry, bumpy, bleeding, fluid and
tightness.

relevance of the PSAAD items:
• There may be certain items that could drive the total score. This reviewer notes, however,
that after reviewing the data, the item-level median values at Baseline and Week 12, as
well as, the median change values from Baseline to Week 12 do not indicate that there
are certain items that are overly influencing the total score. See Section C.7 for item-level
statistics.
Overall, this reviewer does not have any critical concerns regarding the content validity of the
PSAAD.

5.5.5 Other Measurement Properties
The applicant evaluated the psychometric properties of the PSAAD using data from the phase 2b
trial (Study B7451006 5) and two phase 3 trials (Studies B7451012 6 and B74510137).
A summary of the findings is provided for each study. For more details on the methodology and
results of these analyses, see the PRO Evidence Dossier.
Study B7451006
A total of 164 patients in Study B7451006 were included in the analysis population.
The results are summarized as follows:
• The applicant performed a confirmatory factor analysis (CFA) to assess the
unidimensionality of the PSAAD. The standardized loadings for each PSAAD item was
more than 0.4. The Bentler’s Comparative Fit Index (CFI), which was used as the
primary goodness of fit index, was less than 0.90 (ranging from 0.77 to 0.82). The
RMSEA values were >0.05 and the SRMR values were >0.08.
•

Item-level ceiling effects (defined as greater than 18% with response 10 in this document
[twice the expected probability under a uniform distribution] were demonstrated at
baseline.

•

For assessment of internal consistency reliability, Cronbach’s coefficient alpha ranged
from 0.93 to 0.97.

•

For assessment of test-retest reliability, the initial intra-class coefficients (ICC) for all
available daily PSAAD total scores during the 28-day screening period (Week −4
to Week −1) was 0.81.

•

For assessment of convergent validity, moderate to strong correlations (0.67-0.91) were
found between the PSAAD and the PRO assessments (PP-NRS, PtGA, PGIS, DLQI total,
POEM total) measuring similar concepts related to AD symptoms with the PRO
instruments at Week 12, as hypothesized. Additionally, low to moderate correlations
(0.24-0.60) were found between the PSAAD and the ClinRO assessments (IGA, EASI,
BSA, SCORAD) at Week 12, however, not as hypothesized.

•

For assessment of known groups validity, patients in the respective score bands of the
DLQI (0-1 = no effect at all on patient’ life; 2-5 = small effect on patient’s life; 6-10 =
moderate effect on patient’s life; 11-20 = very large effect on patient’s life; 21-30 =
extremely large effect on patient’s life) at all available time points 7 had better PSAAD

Reviewer’s comment(s):
• Missing data was high (56.7% at baseline, 56.1% at Week 2, 59.1% at Week 4, 63.4% at
Week 8, and 65.2% at Week 12), however, because this was a large study there was
sufficient sample size to perform some of the analyses.
•

The fit indices for the CFA indicate a less than adequate fit; however, the results should
be cautiously interpreted due to the small sample size (i.e., the ratio of observations per
parameter was less than 5, a commonly applied rule of thumb). Due to the lack of
interpretability of these results, this reviewer submitted an information request to the
applicant to use their phase 3 data to conduct another CFA. See the CFA results for
Studies B7451012 and B7451013.

•

The applicant notes that the ceiling effects observed were most likely a result of the study
enrollment criteria.

•

In general, the results for internal consistency reliability, test-retest reliability, and
convergent validity (for PRO assessments) is within acceptable and within reasonable
range. The patterns among the correlations between the PSAAD total score and 3 of the 4
clinician-reported measures (IGA, EASI, and BSA) were lower than 0.40. However, the
lower correlation is likely due to the fact that clinical examination of visible atopic
dermatitis in clinic may not be fully reflective of the patient experience on a day to day
basis.

•

This reviewer does not agree that the defined known groups from the DLQI instrument
are appropriate for the assessment of known groups validity as there is limited data to
support the numerical cutoffs. Further, the DLQI instrument has intrinsic limitations.

•

This reviewer notes the following limitations of the PGIC for the assessment of
responsiveness:
o The PGIC generically asks about a “skin condition” and not specific to atopic
dermatitis signs and symptoms.
o The PGIC may be susceptible to recall error.
o While informative, the PGIC may be a less reliable measure of change from baseline
data.

NDA 213871/IND
•

A CFA was performed to confirm the unidimensionality of the PSAAD. The
standardized loadings for each PSAAD item for each study were more than 0.4. Bentler’s
CFI was less than 0.90. The RMSEA values were >0.05 and the SRMR values were
>0.08.

•

For assessment of internal consistency reliability, Cronbach coefficient alphas ranged
from 0.95 to 0.97 in Study B7451012; and 0.94 to 0.97 in Study B7451013.

•

For assessment of test-retest reliability, ICCs for the weekly PSAAD total scores ranged
from 0.86 to 0.95 in Study B7451012; and 0.85 to 0.94 in Study B7451013.

•

For assessment of convergent validity, moderate to strong correlations (0.69-0.87) were
found between the PSAAD and PRO assessments (PP-NRS, PtGA, DLQI total, POEM
total) measuring similar concepts related to AD symptoms with the PRO instruments at
Week 12, as hypothesized. Moderate correlations (0.57-0.69) were found between the
PSAAD and the ClinRO assessments (IGA, EASI, SCORAD) at Week 12, as
hypothesized.

•

For assessment of known groups validity, the mean PSAAD scores observed across the
Patient Global Assessment (PtGA) 9 subgroups showed better PSAAD scores (lower
scores) on average for patient subgroups with clearer levels of AD than subgroups with
greater levels of severity at baseline and Week 12. Significant differences (P < 0.0001)
were found for all comparisons between the PtGA subgroups.

•

For assessment of responsiveness, as the PtGA (or IGA) scores improved, PSAAD total
scores also improved (negative changes); and when PtGA (or IGA) scores worsened the
PSAAD total scores also worsened 10. Statistically significant differences (P < 0.05) were
found for all pairwise comparisons of the mean PSAAD change scores between the PtGA
(or IGA) change scores.

Reviewer’s comment(s):
• In general, the results for internal consistency reliability, test-retest reliability, and
convergent validity is within acceptable and within reasonable range.
•

For the assessment of known groups validity, the applicant formed two PtGA
subgroups at baseline (PtGA ≤ 3 and PtGA = 4) and Week 12 (PtGA ≤ 2 and PtGA ≥
3) to define their known groups, This reviewer does not agree that the defined known
groups from the PtGA should be collapsed as there is no information to support that
the collapsed severities are clinically similar. Further, the PtGA instrument may not
be an appropriate reference measure for this assessment due to the following
limitations.

9

“almost clear [1],” and “clear [0]”)

o The anchor asks patients to assess the severity of their atopic dermatitis with
response options of “almost clear” and “clear”; however, some symptoms of the
disease (e.g., itch, pain) cannot be appropriately evaluated on clearance.
In general, this reviewer concludes that the evidence generated from the psychometric
evaluation of the PSAAD across all three studies supports the reliability and validity of the
instrument.
5.5.6 Interpretation of Meaningful Within-Patient Score Changes
The applicant indicated a range of 2.87 to 3 (on a 0 to 10 scale) as a meaningful within-patient
change threshold in the PSAAD total score based on a 2-point change in the patient-reported
PtGA across Studies B7451012 and B7451013.
The applicant performed the following analyses to support the proposed threshold range for
meaningful within-patient change:
• Anchor-based analyses
o Distribution of change on the Skin Pain NRS by change on PGI-S-AD
o Anchor-based empirical cumulative distribution function and probability density
function curves
• Distribution-based analyses
The PtGA was included in phase 3 trials as an anchor to assist in the interpretation of the
PSAAD total score. The PtGA is a single-item question asking patients how they rate their AD
right now on a 5-point verbal rating scale ranging from “clear” to “severe.”
A summary of results is provided below:
• Correlations between the weekly PSAAD total change from baseline scores and PtGA
change from baseline were greater than 0.50.
• Patients reporting 2-point change in the PtGA had a mean PSAAD total score change
between -2.87 to -3.00.
• Patients reporting 1-point change in the PtGA had a mean PSAAD total score change
between -2.00 to -2.04.
• Patients reporting “Better” in the PGIC (administered in Phase 2b study [Study
B7451006] only) had mean PSAAD total score change of -2.66.
• The distribution-based estimates were considerably lower than the anchor-based
estimates at 1.11 for one-half SD (SD = 2.22) and 0.97 for the standard error of
measurement (i.e., 2.22*sqrt(1-0.81)).
Reviewer’s comment(s):
This reviewer is unable to determine a threshold of clinically meaningful within-patient score
change in PSAAD total score due to the following limitation of the PtGA anchor scale:
The anchor asks patients to assess the severity of their atopic dermatitis with response
options of “almost clear” and “clear.” The term “clearance” is generally used in
relation to the visible signs of atopic dermatitis. In the absence of qualitative data, it is
unclear whether the term “clearance” appropriately evaluates some symptoms of the
disease (e.g., itch, pain).
19

Reference ID: 4816610

COA Tracking ID: C2020502
(b) (4)
NDA 213871/IND
The PGIC administered in the phase 2b study (Study B7451006) also had limitations:
o PGIC generically asks about a “skin condition” and not specific to atopic
dermatitis signs and symptoms.
o The PGIC may be susceptible to recall error.
o While informative, the PGIC may be a less reliable measure of change from
baseline data.
Based on discussion with Clinical and Biostatistics, they consider a proportion of patients
achieving 0 in each item of the PSAAD to be clinically meaningful. The Biostatistics reviewer
performed these analyses and noted that there was a small proportion of participants who
fulfilled this criterion.
Figures 2 to 3 presents PSAAD total change scores from baseline to Week 12 by treatment arms
for Studies B7451012 and B7451013.
